Well… my family says I had to come in because of the money, mostly. I spent our savings on wood and lights and all kinds of display things—stuff to fix up the house, make it right, you know. And they got upset because I kept working even after dark.

But it wasn’t just tinkering around. I felt like if I stopped, the whole house… maybe the whole block… would miss its chance. Like it had to be done then. People don’t always understand that.

I don’t know if I’d put it that way… “can’t stop” sounds a little strong. I mean, if I’m trying to watch the news or read something, my eyes still catch on the wall or the floorboard, and then I remember the tool chest needs sorting—well, not sorting, exactly, it’s that the screwdriver keeps ending up in the wrong drawer.

My father had a little workbench like that, always neat as church on Sunday. I never could keep mine that way. But the house… it’s just hard to ignore when you’re sitting right in the middle of it. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.

Intrusive… I don’t know. That sounds like when something jumps in your head and you don’t want it there. I wouldn’t say it feels exactly like that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… yes, I suppose they are. I mean, fixing that house, the old place over by Schiller Park, that feels like me. It’s not just patching plaster or fooling around with boards.

I know people might not see it yet, but I can make that house into something important, something people would actually come to see. Because the way light falls in a room, and pictures, and where things belong on the walls—most people don’t understand that like I do.

My wife used to say I’d stare at a window like it was talking to me. Maybe she was teasing, but there’s truth in it. So yes, it’s in line with what I want. It feels like what I’m supposed to be doing.

Against my will… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, nobody’s forcing me to think about it, if that’s what you mean.

It’s more like when you’re sitting in a room and there’s a drip from the faucet—you notice it, and then you think, well, that ought to be fixed. My uncle had a place with a faucet like that, drove everybody crazy at Thanksgiving, though that’s not really the same thing.

So… no, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it feeling like it’s against my will exactly. It’s just something my mind goes to.

My father used to say a house talks to you if you listen, and ours has been talking plenty lately. So I move from one thing to the next… I don’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these ideas to fix up the house keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it repeating, exactly. It’s more like… I notice one thing and then it leads to another. Like the porch light, then the steps, then I remember the garden bed needs edging.\n\nMy wife used to say I never finished a project straight through, but that was years ago—back when we still had that old ladder with the bent rung. Anyway, the ideas come around, sure, but I’m not sure what you mean by “repeating in my mind.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I guess… it feels more like I have to, but not like somebody’s got a rope on me. I just can’t sit in the chair anymore. I’ll sit down and then I’m up again—out to the garden, then the darkroom, then the porch—carrying tools around, fixing things before anybody even asks.\n\nAnd the porch, you know, that wood’s been needing attention for years. My brother-in-law said it would hold, but he always said things like that… Anyway, I do want the house right, sure, but it feels like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're thinking about fixing up the house even when you're not actually working on it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think about the house, sure. Hard not to, living in it. You sit there and your eyes land on the trim or the window latch, and then you remember when we painted that room—must’ve been, oh, years ago, because my son was still small then.\n\nBut when I’m not working on it? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there making plans exactly. It’s more like the house is just there, and one thing reminds me of another. Does that count as thinking about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about fixing up the house, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way… “can’t stop” sounds a little strong. I mean, if I’m trying to watch the news or read something, my eyes still catch on the wall or the floorboard, and then I remember the tool chest needs sorting—well, not sorting, exactly, it’s that the screwdriver keeps ending up in the wrong drawer.\n\nMy father had a little workbench like that, always neat as church on Sunday. I never could keep mine that way. But the house… it’s just hard to ignore when you’re sitting right in the middle of it. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about fixing up the house are intrusive, or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: Intrusive… I don’t know. That sounds like when something jumps in your head and you don’t want it there. I wouldn’t say it feels exactly like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there and notice the window frame or the paint peeling, and then my mind goes over to what needs doing. But unwelcome? No, not like a bad memory or something. It’s just… there. The house is there. Like when my wife used to say you can’t walk past a crooked picture without straightening it—well, she said that about her sister mostly, but anyway.\n\nI’m not sure I’m answering it right. I don’t really know if I’d call the thoughts intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are in line with who you are and what you want?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose they are. I mean, fixing that house, the old place over by Schiller Park, that feels like me. It’s not just patching plaster or fooling around with boards.\n\nI know people might not see it yet, but I can make that house into something important, something people would actually come to see. Because the way light falls in a room, and pictures, and where things belong on the walls—most people don’t understand that like I do.\n\nMy wife used to say I’d stare at a window like it was talking to me. Maybe she was teasing, but there’s truth in it. So yes, it’s in line with what I want. It feels like what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about fixing up the house against your will?\n\nPatient: Against my will… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, nobody’s forcing me to think about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like when you’re sitting in a room and there’s a drip from the faucet—you notice it, and then you think, well, that ought to be fixed. My uncle had a place with a faucet like that, drove everybody crazy at Thanksgiving, though that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it feeling like it’s against my will exactly. It’s just something my mind goes to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Would I rather not be thinking about it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’d rather be thinking about lunch, I suppose, or whether the paper got delivered right. The boy who used to throw papers on our street, he had a terrible arm—always in the bushes.

Does that count as thinking about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about fixing up the house, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way… “can’t stop” sounds a little strong. I mean, if I’m trying to watch the news or read something, my eyes still catch on the wall or the floorboard, and then I remember the tool chest needs sorting—well, not sorting, exactly, it’s that the screwdriver keeps ending up in the wrong drawer.\n\nMy father had a little workbench like that, always neat as church on Sunday. I never could keep mine that way. But the house… it’s just hard to ignore when you’re sitting right in the middle of it. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about fixing up the house are intrusive, or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: Intrusive… I don’t know. That sounds like when something jumps in your head and you don’t want it there. I wouldn’t say it feels exactly like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there and notice the window frame or the paint peeling, and then my mind goes over to what needs doing. But unwelcome? No, not like a bad memory or something. It’s just… there. The house is there. Like when my wife used to say you can’t walk past a crooked picture without straightening it—well, she said that about her sister mostly, but anyway.\n\nI’m not sure I’m answering it right. I don’t really know if I’d call the thoughts intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are in line with who you are and what you want?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose they are. I mean, fixing that house, the old place over by Schiller Park, that feels like me. It’s not just patching plaster or fooling around with boards.\n\nI know people might not see it yet, but I can make that house into something important, something people would actually come to see. Because the way light falls in a room, and pictures, and where things belong on the walls—most people don’t understand that like I do.\n\nMy wife used to say I’d stare at a window like it was talking to me. Maybe she was teasing, but there’s truth in it. So yes, it’s in line with what I want. It feels like what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about fixing up the house against your will?\n\nPatient: Against my will… I’m not sure I’d say that. I haven’t noticed it feeling like it’s against my will exactly. It’s just something my mind goes to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are something that you would rather not be thinking about?\n\nPatient: Would I rather not be thinking about it… I don’t know. Anxious if I didn’t… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Uneasy, maybe, but that could just be because nobody likes leaving a job half-done. My father used to say if you start sanding a door and walk away, the whole room knows it—funny thing to say, but I still remember it.

I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about fixing up the house are intrusive, or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: Intrusive… I don’t know. That sounds like when something jumps in your head and you don’t want it there. I wouldn’t say it feels exactly like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there and notice the window frame or the paint peeling, and then my mind goes over to what needs doing. But unwelcome? No, not like a bad memory or something. It’s just… there. The house is there. Like when my wife used to say you can’t walk past a crooked picture without straightening it—well, she said that about her sister mostly, but anyway.\n\nI’m not sure I’m answering it right. I don’t really know if I’d call the thoughts intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are in line with who you are and what you want?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose they are. A specific way or order… I don’t know. I mean, some things have to come before others, don’t they? You can’t paint before you scrape, or at least that’s what the fellow at the hardware store told me years ago—nice man, had a little dog that sat by the register.

I just look at what needs doing and start somewhere, I suppose. Sometimes I get sidetracked, though, because once you move one thing, you see another thing behind it.

I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there making plans exactly. It’s more like the house is just there, and one thing reminds me of another. Does that count as thinking about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about fixing up the house, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way… “can’t stop” sounds a little strong. I mean, if I’m trying to watch the news or read something, my eyes still catch on the wall or the floorboard, and then I remember the tool chest needs sorting—well, not sorting, exactly, it’s that the screwdriver keeps ending up in the wrong drawer.\n\nMy father had a little workbench like that, always neat as church on Sunday. I never could keep mine that way. But the house… it’s just hard to ignore when you’re sitting right in the middle of it. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about fixing up the house are intrusive, or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: Intrusive… I don’t know. That sounds like when something jumps in your head and you don’t want it there. I wouldn’t say it feels exactly like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there and notice the window frame or the paint peeling, and then my mind goes over to what needs doing. But unwelcome? No, not like a bad memory or something. It’s just… there. The house is there. Like when my wife used to say you can’t walk past a crooked picture without straightening it—well, she said that about her sister mostly, but anyway.\n\nI’m not sure I’m answering it right. I don’t really know if I’d call the thoughts intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are in line with who you are and what you want?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose they are. I mean, fixing that house, the old place over by Schiller Park, that feels like me. It’s not just patching plaster or fooling around with boards.\n\nI know people might not see it yet, but I can make that house into something important, something people would actually come to see. Because the way light falls in a room, and pictures, and where things belong on the walls—most people don’t understand that like I do.\n\nMy wife used to say I’d stare at a window like it was talking to me. Maybe she was teasing, but there’s truth in it. So yes, it’s in line with what I want. It feels like what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about fixing up the house against your will?\n\nPatient: Against my will… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, nobody’s forcing me to think about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like when you’re sitting in a room and there’s a drip from the faucet—you notice it, and then you think, well, that ought to be fixed. My uncle had a place with a faucet like that, drove everybody crazy at Thanksgiving, though that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it feeling like it’s against my will exactly. It’s just something my mind goes to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are something that you would rather not be thinking about?\n\nPatient: Would I rather not be thinking about it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’d rather be thinking about lunch, I suppose, or whether the paper got delivered right. The boy who used to throw papers on our street, he had a terrible arm—always in the bushes.\n\nBut the house thoughts, they’re not exactly something I’m pushing away. I can’t say I sit there wishing they’d stop. It’s more like they come along because the place needs seeing to. I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you'd feel anxious or uneasy if you didn't do them?\n\nPatient: Anxious if I didn’t… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Uneasy, maybe, but that could just be because nobody likes leaving a job half-done. My father used to say if you start sanding a door and walk away, the whole room knows it—funny thing to say, but I still remember it.\n\nI don’t think I’m doing it because I’m scared something will happen if I don’t. It’s more… the house is there, and it needs what it needs. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m anxious, so I better do this.” It just seems like the thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these tasks in a specific way or order to feel right about fixing up the house?\n\nPatient: A specific way or order… I don’t know. I mean, some things have to come before others, don’t they? You can’t paint before you scrape, or at least that’s what the fellow at the hardware store told me years ago—nice man, had a little dog that sat by the register.\n\nBut if you mean like I have to do it just so or it feels wrong in my head… I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t think there’s some special order I’m following. I just look at what needs doing and start somewhere, I suppose. Sometimes I get sidetracked, though, because once you move one thing, you see another thing behind it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Perfect? I don’t know about perfect. That’s a big word. Houses are never perfect, not really—not old ones anyway. You fix one corner and then the window starts sticking, or the porch step squeaks again. My uncle had a place like that, always smelled like oilcloth and rain when you walked in.

I don’t think I’m trying to make it perfect, exactly. I just see things that need doing, and then I go after them. Maybe I like it looking right, but perfect… no, I can’t say I’ve thought of it like that.

I can’t just sit in the chair anymore. I’ll try, but then I see something—out in the garden, or the porch needs fixing, or I remember something in the darkroom—and I’m up again, carrying tools around, doing it before anyone even asks.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make trouble. My father used to say a house talks to you if you listen, and ours has been talking plenty lately. So I move from one thing to the next… I don’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these ideas to fix up the house keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it repeating, exactly. It’s more like… I notice one thing and then it leads to another. Like the porch light, then the steps, then I remember the garden bed needs edging.\n\nMy wife used to say I never finished a project straight through, but that was years ago—back when we still had that old ladder with the bent rung. Anyway, the ideas come around, sure, but I’m not sure what you mean by “repeating in my mind.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I guess… it feels more like I have to, but not like somebody’s got a rope on me. I just can’t sit in the chair anymore. I’ll sit down and then I’m up again—out to the garden, then the darkroom, then the porch—carrying tools around, fixing things before anybody even asks.\n\nAnd the porch, you know, that wood’s been needing attention for years. My brother-in-law said it would hold, but he always said things like that… Anyway, I do want the house right, sure, but it feels like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're thinking about fixing up the house even when you're not actually working on it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think about the house, sure. Hard not to, living in it. You sit there and your eyes land on the trim or the window latch, and then you remember when we painted that room—must’ve been, oh, years ago, because my son was still small then.\n\nBut when I’m not working on it? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there making plans exactly. It’s more like the house is just there, and one thing reminds me of another. Does that count as thinking about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about fixing up the house, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way… “can’t stop” sounds a little strong. I mean, if I’m trying to watch the news or read something, my eyes still catch on the wall or the floorboard, and then I remember the tool chest needs sorting—well, not sorting, exactly, it’s that the screwdriver keeps ending up in the wrong drawer.\n\nMy father had a little workbench like that, always neat as church on Sunday. I never could keep mine that way. But the house… it’s just hard to ignore when you’re sitting right in the middle of it. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about fixing up the house are intrusive, or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: Intrusive… I don’t know. That sounds like when something jumps in your head and you don’t want it there. I wouldn’t say it feels exactly like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there and notice the window frame or the paint peeling, and then my mind goes over to what needs doing. But unwelcome? No, not like a bad memory or something. It’s just… there. The house is there. Like when my wife used to say you can’t walk past a crooked picture without straightening it—well, she said that about her sister mostly, but anyway.\n\nI’m not sure I’m answering it right. I don’t really know if I’d call the thoughts intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are in line with who you are and what you want?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose they are. I mean, fixing that house, the old place over by Schiller Park, that feels like me. It’s not just patching plaster or fooling around with boards.\n\nI know people might not see it yet, but I can make that house into something important, something people would actually come to see. Because the way light falls in a room, and pictures, and where things belong on the walls—most people don’t understand that like I do.\n\nMy wife used to say I’d stare at a window like it was talking to me. Maybe she was teasing, but there’s truth in it. So yes, it’s in line with what I want. It feels like what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about fixing up the house against your will?\n\nPatient: Against my will… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, nobody’s forcing me to think about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like when you’re sitting in a room and there’s a drip from the faucet—you notice it, and then you think, well, that ought to be fixed. My uncle had a place with a faucet like that, drove everybody crazy at Thanksgiving, though that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it feeling like it’s against my will exactly. It’s just something my mind goes to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are something that you would rather not be thinking about?\n\nPatient: Would I rather not be thinking about it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’d rather be thinking about lunch, I suppose, or whether the paper got delivered right. The boy who used to throw papers on our street, he had a terrible arm—always in the bushes.\n\nBut the house thoughts, they’re not exactly something I’m pushing away. I can’t say I sit there wishing they’d stop. It’s more like they come along because the place needs seeing to. I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you'd feel anxious or uneasy if you didn't do them?\n\nPatient: Anxious if I didn’t… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Uneasy, maybe, but that could just be because nobody likes leaving a job half-done. My father used to say if you start sanding a door and walk away, the whole room knows it—funny thing to say, but I still remember it.\n\nI don’t think I’m doing it because I’m scared something will happen if I don’t. It’s more… the house is there, and it needs what it needs. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m anxious, so I better do this.” It just seems like the thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these tasks in a specific way or order to feel right about fixing up the house?\n\nPatient: A specific way or order… I don’t know. I mean, some things have to come before others, don’t they? You can’t paint before you scrape, or at least that’s what the fellow at the hardware store told me years ago—nice man, had a little dog that sat by the register.\n\nBut if you mean like I have to do it just so or it feels wrong in my head… I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t think there’s some special order I’m following. I just look at what needs doing and start somewhere, I suppose. Sometimes I get sidetracked, though, because once you move one thing, you see another thing behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the house has to be perfect, or that you're trying to make it perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect? I don’t know about perfect. That’s a big word. Houses are never perfect, not really—not old ones anyway. You fix one corner and then the window starts sticking, or the porch step squeaks again. My uncle had a place like that, always smelled like oilcloth and rain when you walked in.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to make it perfect, exactly. I just see things that need doing, and then I go after them. Maybe I like it looking right, but perfect… no, I can’t say I’ve thought of it like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F42.2", "F60.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Extremely upset? I don’t know… I might notice if they did something funny, like put the hinge on crooked or use the wrong kind of paint. My neighbor once painted a fence with indoor paint, and it peeled something awful after the first rain—looked like sunburn, almost.

But if somebody fixed the house and it wasn’t how I’d do it… I can’t say I’d be extremely upset. Maybe I’d grumble a little, same as anyone. I haven’t really had that happen in a way where I’d know.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my family says I had to come in because of the money, mostly. I spent our savings on wood and lights and all kinds of display things—stuff to fix up the house, make it right, you know. And they got upset because I kept working even after dark.\n\nBut it wasn’t just tinkering around. I felt like if I stopped, the whole house… maybe the whole block… would miss its chance. Like it had to be done then. People don’t always understand that.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to do these things to fix up the house, like you couldn't stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it feels that way. I can’t just sit in the chair anymore. I’ll try, but then I see something—out in the garden, or the porch needs fixing, or I remember something in the darkroom—and I’m up again, carrying tools around, doing it before anyone even asks.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make trouble. My father used to say a house talks to you if you listen, and ours has been talking plenty lately. So I move from one thing to the next… I don’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these ideas to fix up the house keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it repeating, exactly. It’s more like… I notice one thing and then it leads to another. Like the porch light, then the steps, then I remember the garden bed needs edging.\n\nMy wife used to say I never finished a project straight through, but that was years ago—back when we still had that old ladder with the bent rung. Anyway, the ideas come around, sure, but I’m not sure what you mean by “repeating in my mind.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I guess… it feels more like I have to, but not like somebody’s got a rope on me. I just can’t sit in the chair anymore. I’ll sit down and then I’m up again—out to the garden, then the darkroom, then the porch—carrying tools around, fixing things before anybody even asks.\n\nAnd the porch, you know, that wood’s been needing attention for years. My brother-in-law said it would hold, but he always said things like that… Anyway, I do want the house right, sure, but it feels like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're thinking about fixing up the house even when you're not actually working on it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think about the house, sure. Hard not to, living in it. You sit there and your eyes land on the trim or the window latch, and then you remember when we painted that room—must’ve been, oh, years ago, because my son was still small then.\n\nBut when I’m not working on it? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there making plans exactly. It’s more like the house is just there, and one thing reminds me of another. Does that count as thinking about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about fixing up the house, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way… “can’t stop” sounds a little strong. I mean, if I’m trying to watch the news or read something, my eyes still catch on the wall or the floorboard, and then I remember the tool chest needs sorting—well, not sorting, exactly, it’s that the screwdriver keeps ending up in the wrong drawer.\n\nMy father had a little workbench like that, always neat as church on Sunday. I never could keep mine that way. But the house… it’s just hard to ignore when you’re sitting right in the middle of it. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about fixing up the house are intrusive, or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: Intrusive… I don’t know. That sounds like when something jumps in your head and you don’t want it there. I wouldn’t say it feels exactly like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there and notice the window frame or the paint peeling, and then my mind goes over to what needs doing. But unwelcome? No, not like a bad memory or something. It’s just… there. The house is there. Like when my wife used to say you can’t walk past a crooked picture without straightening it—well, she said that about her sister mostly, but anyway.\n\nI’m not sure I’m answering it right. I don’t really know if I’d call the thoughts intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are in line with who you are and what you want?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose they are. I mean, fixing that house, the old place over by Schiller Park, that feels like me. It’s not just patching plaster or fooling around with boards.\n\nI know people might not see it yet, but I can make that house into something important, something people would actually come to see. Because the way light falls in a room, and pictures, and where things belong on the walls—most people don’t understand that like I do.\n\nMy wife used to say I’d stare at a window like it was talking to me. Maybe she was teasing, but there’s truth in it. So yes, it’s in line with what I want. It feels like what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about fixing up the house against your will?\n\nPatient: Against my will… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, nobody’s forcing me to think about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like when you’re sitting in a room and there’s a drip from the faucet—you notice it, and then you think, well, that ought to be fixed. My uncle had a place with a faucet like that, drove everybody crazy at Thanksgiving, though that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it feeling like it’s against my will exactly. It’s just something my mind goes to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are something that you would rather not be thinking about?\n\nPatient: Would I rather not be thinking about it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’d rather be thinking about lunch, I suppose, or whether the paper got delivered right. The boy who used to throw papers on our street, he had a terrible arm—always in the bushes.\n\nBut the house thoughts, they’re not exactly something I’m pushing away. I can’t say I sit there wishing they’d stop. It’s more like they come along because the place needs seeing to. I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you'd feel anxious or uneasy if you didn't do them?\n\nPatient: Anxious if I didn’t… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Uneasy, maybe, but that could just be because nobody likes leaving a job half-done. My father used to say if you start sanding a door and walk away, the whole room knows it—funny thing to say, but I still remember it.\n\nI don’t think I’m doing it because I’m scared something will happen if I don’t. It’s more… the house is there, and it needs what it needs. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m anxious, so I better do this.” It just seems like the thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these tasks in a specific way or order to feel right about fixing up the house?\n\nPatient: A specific way or order… I don’t know. I mean, some things have to come before others, don’t they? You can’t paint before you scrape, or at least that’s what the fellow at the hardware store told me years ago—nice man, had a little dog that sat by the register.\n\nBut if you mean like I have to do it just so or it feels wrong in my head… I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t think there’s some special order I’m following. I just look at what needs doing and start somewhere, I suppose. Sometimes I get sidetracked, though, because once you move one thing, you see another thing behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the house has to be perfect, or that you're trying to make it perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect? I don’t know about perfect. That’s a big word. Houses are never perfect, not really—not old ones anyway. You fix one corner and then the window starts sticking, or the porch step squeaks again. My uncle had a place like that, always smelled like oilcloth and rain when you walked in.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to make it perfect, exactly. I just see things that need doing, and then I go after them. Maybe I like it looking right, but perfect… no, I can’t say I’ve thought of it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be extremely upset if someone else were to fix up the house and it wasn't done exactly the way you would do it?\n\nPatient: Extremely upset? I don’t know… I might notice if they did something funny, like put the hinge on crooked or use the wrong kind of paint. My neighbor once painted a fence with indoor paint, and it peeled something awful after the first rain—looked like sunburn, almost.\n\nBut if somebody fixed the house and it wasn’t how I’d do it… I can’t say I’d be extremely upset. Maybe I’d grumble a little, same as anyone. I haven’t really had that happen in a way where I’d know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My neighbor once painted a fence with indoor paint, and it peeled something awful after the first rain—looked like sunburn, almost.\n\nBut if somebody fixed the house and it wasn’t how I’d do it… I can’t say I’d be extremely upset. Maybe I’d grumble a little, same as anyone. I haven’t really had that happen in a way where I’d know.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you need to be in charge of fixing up the house yourself, rather than letting someone else do it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I suppose I do feel like I ought to be the one doing it, yes. Not because other people can’t swing a hammer—some of them can do that fine—but they don’t see it the way I see it.

That old Schiller Park house, it could be something important. People could come look at it, you know, the way the light comes through the windows and hits the walls, the pictures, the whole feeling of the place. Most folks just slap paint on and call it done. They don’t understand light and pictures the way I do. So yes, I guess I’d rather be in charge of it myself.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my family says I had to come in because of the money, mostly. I spent our savings on wood and lights and all kinds of display things—stuff to fix up the house, make it right, you know. And they got upset because I kept working even after dark.\n\nBut it wasn’t just tinkering around. I felt like if I stopped, the whole house… maybe the whole block… would miss its chance. Like it had to be done then. People don’t always understand that.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to do these things to fix up the house, like you couldn't stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it feels that way. I can’t just sit in the chair anymore. I’ll try, but then I see something—out in the garden, or the porch needs fixing, or I remember something in the darkroom—and I’m up again, carrying tools around, doing it before anyone even asks.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make trouble. My father used to say a house talks to you if you listen, and ours has been talking plenty lately. So I move from one thing to the next… I don’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these ideas to fix up the house keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it repeating, exactly. It’s more like… I notice one thing and then it leads to another. Like the porch light, then the steps, then I remember the garden bed needs edging.\n\nMy wife used to say I never finished a project straight through, but that was years ago—back when we still had that old ladder with the bent rung. Anyway, the ideas come around, sure, but I’m not sure what you mean by “repeating in my mind.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I guess… it feels more like I have to, but not like somebody’s got a rope on me. I just can’t sit in the chair anymore. I’ll sit down and then I’m up again—out to the garden, then the darkroom, then the porch—carrying tools around, fixing things before anybody even asks.\n\nAnd the porch, you know, that wood’s been needing attention for years. My brother-in-law said it would hold, but he always said things like that… Anyway, I do want the house right, sure, but it feels like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're thinking about fixing up the house even when you're not actually working on it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think about the house, sure. Hard not to, living in it. You sit there and your eyes land on the trim or the window latch, and then you remember when we painted that room—must’ve been, oh, years ago, because my son was still small then.\n\nBut when I’m not working on it? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there making plans exactly. It’s more like the house is just there, and one thing reminds me of another. Does that count as thinking about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about fixing up the house, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way… “can’t stop” sounds a little strong. I mean, if I’m trying to watch the news or read something, my eyes still catch on the wall or the floorboard, and then I remember the tool chest needs sorting—well, not sorting, exactly, it’s that the screwdriver keeps ending up in the wrong drawer.\n\nMy father had a little workbench like that, always neat as church on Sunday. I never could keep mine that way. But the house… it’s just hard to ignore when you’re sitting right in the middle of it. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about fixing up the house are intrusive, or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: Intrusive… I don’t know. That sounds like when something jumps in your head and you don’t want it there. I wouldn’t say it feels exactly like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there and notice the window frame or the paint peeling, and then my mind goes over to what needs doing. But unwelcome? No, not like a bad memory or something. It’s just… there. The house is there. Like when my wife used to say you can’t walk past a crooked picture without straightening it—well, she said that about her sister mostly, but anyway.\n\nI’m not sure I’m answering it right. I don’t really know if I’d call the thoughts intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are in line with who you are and what you want?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose they are. I mean, fixing that house, the old place over by Schiller Park, that feels like me. It’s not just patching plaster or fooling around with boards.\n\nI know people might not see it yet, but I can make that house into something important, something people would actually come to see. Because the way light falls in a room, and pictures, and where things belong on the walls—most people don’t understand that like I do.\n\nMy wife used to say I’d stare at a window like it was talking to me. Maybe she was teasing, but there’s truth in it. So yes, it’s in line with what I want. It feels like what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about fixing up the house against your will?\n\nPatient: Against my will… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, nobody’s forcing me to think about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like when you’re sitting in a room and there’s a drip from the faucet—you notice it, and then you think, well, that ought to be fixed. My uncle had a place with a faucet like that, drove everybody crazy at Thanksgiving, though that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it feeling like it’s against my will exactly. It’s just something my mind goes to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are something that you would rather not be thinking about?\n\nPatient: Would I rather not be thinking about it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’d rather be thinking about lunch, I suppose, or whether the paper got delivered right. The boy who used to throw papers on our street, he had a terrible arm—always in the bushes.\n\nBut the house thoughts, they’re not exactly something I’m pushing away. I can’t say I sit there wishing they’d stop. It’s more like they come along because the place needs seeing to. I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you'd feel anxious or uneasy if you didn't do them?\n\nPatient: Anxious if I didn’t… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Uneasy, maybe, but that could just be because nobody likes leaving a job half-done. My father used to say if you start sanding a door and walk away, the whole room knows it—funny thing to say, but I still remember it.\n\nI don’t think I’m doing it because I’m scared something will happen if I don’t. It’s more… the house is there, and it needs what it needs. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m anxious, so I better do this.” It just seems like the thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these tasks in a specific way or order to feel right about fixing up the house?\n\nPatient: A specific way or order… I don’t know. I mean, some things have to come before others, don’t they? You can’t paint before you scrape, or at least that’s what the fellow at the hardware store told me years ago—nice man, had a little dog that sat by the register.\n\nBut if you mean like I have to do it just so or it feels wrong in my head… I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t think there’s some special order I’m following. I just look at what needs doing and start somewhere, I suppose. Sometimes I get sidetracked, though, because once you move one thing, you see another thing behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the house has to be perfect, or that you're trying to make it perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect? I don’t know about perfect. That’s a big word. Houses are never perfect, not really—not old ones anyway. You fix one corner and then the window starts sticking, or the porch step squeaks again. My uncle had a place like that, always smelled like oilcloth and rain when you walked in.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to make it perfect, exactly. I haven’t really had that happen in a way where I’d know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of fixing up the house yourself, rather than letting someone else do it?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I do feel like I ought to be the one doing it, yes. Control… that’s a funny word. I mean, I can stop to eat if someone puts a plate in front of me, I’m not a machine. But once I see what needs doing, it gets in my head.

They say I’m here because I spent our savings on lumber and lights and those display things, and because I kept working after dark. But it didn’t feel like wasting it. It felt like if I stopped, the house would miss its chance—maybe the whole block would. Like when a train comes through and you either get on or you don’t. So… I guess maybe it was pulling me along more than I like to admit.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my family says I had to come in because of the money, mostly. I spent our savings on wood and lights and all kinds of display things—stuff to fix up the house, make it right, you know. Like it had to be done then. People don’t always understand that.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to do these things to fix up the house, like you couldn't stop yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it feels that way. I can’t just sit in the chair anymore. My father used to say a house talks to you if you listen, and ours has been talking plenty lately. So I move from one thing to the next… I don’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these ideas to fix up the house keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it repeating, exactly. It’s more like… I notice one thing and then it leads to another. Like the porch light, then the steps, then I remember the garden bed needs edging.\n\nMy wife used to say I never finished a project straight through, but that was years ago—back when we still had that old ladder with the bent rung. Anyway, the ideas come around, sure, but I’m not sure what you mean by “repeating in my mind.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I guess… it feels more like I have to, but not like somebody’s got a rope on me. I’ll sit down and then I’m up again—out to the garden, then the darkroom, then the porch—carrying tools around, fixing things before anybody even asks.\n\nAnd the porch, you know, that wood’s been needing attention for years. My brother-in-law said it would hold, but he always said things like that… Anyway, I do want the house right, sure, but it feels like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're thinking about fixing up the house even when you're not actually working on it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I think about the house, sure. Hard not to, living in it. You sit there and your eyes land on the trim or the window latch, and then you remember when we painted that room—must’ve been, oh, years ago, because my son was still small then.\n\nBut when I’m not working on it? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there making plans exactly. It’s more like the house is just there, and one thing reminds me of another. Does that count as thinking about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop thinking about fixing up the house, even when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way… “can’t stop” sounds a little strong. I mean, if I’m trying to watch the news or read something, my eyes still catch on the wall or the floorboard, and then I remember the tool chest needs sorting—well, not sorting, exactly, it’s that the screwdriver keeps ending up in the wrong drawer.\n\nMy father had a little workbench like that, always neat as church on Sunday. I never could keep mine that way. But the house… it’s just hard to ignore when you’re sitting right in the middle of it. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about fixing up the house are intrusive, or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: Intrusive… I don’t know. That sounds like when something jumps in your head and you don’t want it there. I wouldn’t say it feels exactly like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there and notice the window frame or the paint peeling, and then my mind goes over to what needs doing. But unwelcome? No, not like a bad memory or something. It’s just… there. The house is there. Like when my wife used to say you can’t walk past a crooked picture without straightening it—well, she said that about her sister mostly, but anyway.\n\nI’m not sure I’m answering it right. I don’t really know if I’d call the thoughts intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are in line with who you are and what you want?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose they are. I mean, fixing that house, the old place over by Schiller Park, that feels like me. It’s not just patching plaster or fooling around with boards.\n\nI know people might not see it yet, but I can make that house into something important, something people would actually come to see. Because the way light falls in a room, and pictures, and where things belong on the walls—most people don’t understand that like I do.\n\nMy wife used to say I’d stare at a window like it was talking to me. Maybe she was teasing, but there’s truth in it. So yes, it’s in line with what I want. It feels like what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about fixing up the house against your will?\n\nPatient: Against my will… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, nobody’s forcing me to think about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like when you’re sitting in a room and there’s a drip from the faucet—you notice it, and then you think, well, that ought to be fixed. My uncle had a place with a faucet like that, drove everybody crazy at Thanksgiving, though that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it feeling like it’s against my will exactly. It’s just something my mind goes to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are something that you would rather not be thinking about?\n\nPatient: Would I rather not be thinking about it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’d rather be thinking about lunch, I suppose, or whether the paper got delivered right. The boy who used to throw papers on our street, he had a terrible arm—always in the bushes.\n\nBut the house thoughts, they’re not exactly something I’m pushing away. I can’t say I sit there wishing they’d stop. It’s more like they come along because the place needs seeing to. I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you'd feel anxious or uneasy if you didn't do them?\n\nPatient: Anxious if I didn’t… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Uneasy, maybe, but that could just be because nobody likes leaving a job half-done. My father used to say if you start sanding a door and walk away, the whole room knows it—funny thing to say, but I still remember it.\n\nI don’t think I’m doing it because I’m scared something will happen if I don’t. It’s more… the house is there, and it needs what it needs. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m anxious, so I better do this.” It just seems like the thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these tasks in a specific way or order to feel right about fixing up the house?\n\nPatient: A specific way or order… I don’t know. I mean, some things have to come before others, don’t they? You can’t paint before you scrape, or at least that’s what the fellow at the hardware store told me years ago—nice man, had a little dog that sat by the register.\n\nBut if you mean like I have to do it just so or it feels wrong in my head… I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t think there’s some special order I’m following. I just look at what needs doing and start somewhere, I suppose. Sometimes I get sidetracked, though, because once you move one thing, you see another thing behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the house has to be perfect, or that you're trying to make it perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect? I don’t know about perfect. That’s a big word. Houses are never perfect, not really—not old ones anyway. You fix one corner and then the window starts sticking, or the porch step squeaks again. I mean, if I’m trying to watch the news or read something, my eyes still catch on the wall or the floorboard, and then I remember the tool chest needs sorting—well, not sorting, exactly, it’s that the screwdriver keeps ending up in the wrong drawer.\n\nMy father had a little workbench like that, always neat as church on Sunday. I never could keep mine that way. But the house… it’s just hard to ignore when you’re sitting right in the middle of it. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thoughts about fixing up the house are intrusive, or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: Intrusive… I don’t know. That sounds like when something jumps in your head and you don’t want it there. I wouldn’t say it feels exactly like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there and notice the window frame or the paint peeling, and then my mind goes over to what needs doing. But unwelcome? No, not like a bad memory or something. It’s just… there. The house is there. Like when my wife used to say you can’t walk past a crooked picture without straightening it—well, she said that about her sister mostly, but anyway.\n\nI’m not sure I’m answering it right. I don’t really know if I’d call the thoughts intrusive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are in line with who you are and what you want?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose they are. I mean, fixing that house, the old place over by Schiller Park, that feels like me. It’s not just patching plaster or fooling around with boards.\n\nI know people might not see it yet, but I can make that house into something important, something people would actually come to see. Because the way light falls in a room, and pictures, and where things belong on the walls—most people don’t understand that like I do.\n\nMy wife used to say I’d stare at a window like it was talking to me. Maybe she was teasing, but there’s truth in it. So yes, it’s in line with what I want. It feels like what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts about fixing up the house against your will?\n\nPatient: Against my will… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, nobody’s forcing me to think about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like when you’re sitting in a room and there’s a drip from the faucet—you notice it, and then you think, well, that ought to be fixed. My uncle had a place with a faucet like that, drove everybody crazy at Thanksgiving, though that’s not really the same thing.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it feeling like it’s against my will exactly. It’s just something my mind goes to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are something that you would rather not be thinking about?\n\nPatient: Would I rather not be thinking about it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’d rather be thinking about lunch, I suppose, or whether the paper got delivered right. The boy who used to throw papers on our street, he had a terrible arm—always in the bushes.\n\nBut the house thoughts, they’re not exactly something I’m pushing away. I can’t say I sit there wishing they’d stop. It’s more like they come along because the place needs seeing to. I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks to fix up the house because you'd feel anxious or uneasy if you didn't do them?\n\nPatient: Anxious if I didn’t… I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Uneasy, maybe, but that could just be because nobody likes leaving a job half-done. My father used to say if you start sanding a door and walk away, the whole room knows it—funny thing to say, but I still remember it.\n\nI don’t think I’m doing it because I’m scared something will happen if I don’t. It’s more… the house is there, and it needs what it needs. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m anxious, so I better do this.” It just seems like the thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these tasks in a specific way or order to feel right about fixing up the house?\n\nPatient: A specific way or order… I don’t know. I mean, some things have to come before others, don’t they? You can’t paint before you scrape, or at least that’s what the fellow at the hardware store told me years ago—nice man, had a little dog that sat by the register.\n\nBut if you mean like I have to do it just so or it feels wrong in my head… I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t think there’s some special order I’m following. I just look at what needs doing and start somewhere, I suppose. Sometimes I get sidetracked, though, because once you move one thing, you see another thing behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the house has to be perfect, or that you're trying to make it perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect? I don’t know about perfect. That’s a big word. Houses are never perfect, not really—not old ones anyway. You fix one corner and then the window starts sticking, or the porch step squeaks again. My uncle had a place like that, always smelled like oilcloth and rain when you walked in.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to make it perfect, exactly. I just see things that need doing, and then I go after them. Maybe I like it looking right, but perfect… no, I can’t say I’ve thought of it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be extremely upset if someone else were to fix up the house and it wasn't done exactly the way you would do it?\n\nPatient: Extremely upset? I don’t know… I might notice if they did something funny, like put the hinge on crooked or use the wrong kind of paint. My neighbor once painted a fence with indoor paint, and it peeled something awful after the first rain—looked like sunburn, almost.\n\nBut if somebody fixed the house and it wasn’t how I’d do it… I can’t say I’d be extremely upset. Maybe I’d grumble a little, same as anyone. I haven’t really had that happen in a way where I’d know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of fixing up the house yourself, rather than letting someone else do it?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I do feel like I ought to be the one doing it, yes. Not because other people can’t swing a hammer—some of them can do that fine—but they don’t see it the way I see it.\n\nThat old Schiller Park house, it could be something important. People could come look at it, you know, the way the light comes through the windows and hits the walls, the pictures, the whole feeling of the place. Most folks just slap paint on and call it done. They don’t understand light and pictures the way I do. So yes, I guess I’d rather be in charge of it myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are something that you can control, or do they feel like they're controlling you?\n\nPatient: Control… that’s a funny word. I mean, I can stop to eat if someone puts a plate in front of me, I’m not a machine. But once I see what needs doing, it gets in my head.\n\nThey say I’m here because I spent our savings on lumber and lights and those display things, and because I kept working after dark. But it didn’t feel like wasting it. It felt like if I stopped, the house would miss its chance—maybe the whole block would. Like when a train comes through and you either get on or you don’t. So… I guess maybe it was pulling me along more than I like to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I can see it a certain way, and once I see it, it’s hard to accept it being made ordinary.

I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m anxious, so I better do this.” It just seems like the thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these tasks in a specific way or order to feel right about fixing up the house?\n\nPatient: A specific way or order… I don’t know. You can’t paint before you scrape, or at least that’s what the fellow at the hardware store told me years ago—nice man, had a little dog that sat by the register.\n\nBut if you mean like I have to do it just so or it feels wrong in my head… I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t think there’s some special order I’m following. I just look at what needs doing and start somewhere, I suppose. Sometimes I get sidetracked, though, because once you move one thing, you see another thing behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the house has to be perfect, or that you're trying to make it perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect? I don’t know about perfect. That’s a big word. Houses are never perfect, not really—not old ones anyway. You fix one corner and then the window starts sticking, or the porch step squeaks again. My uncle had a place like that, always smelled like oilcloth and rain when you walked in.\n\nI don’t think I’m trying to make it perfect, exactly. I just see things that need doing, and then I go after them. Maybe I like it looking right, but perfect… no, I can’t say I’ve thought of it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be extremely upset if someone else were to fix up the house and it wasn't done exactly the way you would do it?\n\nPatient: Extremely upset? I don’t know… I might notice if they did something funny, like put the hinge on crooked or use the wrong kind of paint. My neighbor once painted a fence with indoor paint, and it peeled something awful after the first rain—looked like sunburn, almost.\n\nBut if somebody fixed the house and it wasn’t how I’d do it… I can’t say I’d be extremely upset. Maybe I’d grumble a little, same as anyone. I haven’t really had that happen in a way where I’d know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of fixing up the house yourself, rather than letting someone else do it?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I do feel like I ought to be the one doing it, yes. Not because other people can’t swing a hammer—some of them can do that fine—but they don’t see it the way I see it.\n\nThat old Schiller Park house, it could be something important. People could come look at it, you know, the way the light comes through the windows and hits the walls, the pictures, the whole feeling of the place. Most folks just slap paint on and call it done. They don’t understand light and pictures the way I do. So yes, I guess I’d rather be in charge of it myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about fixing up the house are something that you can control, or do they feel like they're controlling you?\n\nPatient: Control… that’s a funny word. I mean, I can stop to eat if someone puts a plate in front of me, I’m not a machine. But once I see what needs doing, it gets in my head.\n\nThey say I’m here because I spent our savings on lumber and lights and those display things, and because I kept working after dark. But it didn’t feel like wasting it. It felt like if I stopped, the house would miss its chance—maybe the whole block would. Like when a train comes through and you either get on or you don’t. So… I guess maybe it was pulling me along more than I like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your efforts to fix up the house are driven by a desire to have everything exactly as you envision it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I can see it a certain way, and once I see it, it’s hard to accept it being made ordinary.\n\nThat house in Schiller Park, it isn’t just some old place with bad boards and dusty corners. I honestly think I can make it into something people would come look at. The light in there—if you hang the pictures right, if you understand how it falls across the wall—it could matter. Most people don’t see that. They just see paint and nails. I see the whole thing. So yes, I want it done the way I see it, because I don’t think anyone else really understands light and pictures the way I do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d put it that way… unwanted thoughts or feelings. I mean, everybody’s got things they don’t like thinking about, I suppose. Bills, old arguments, the way a room smells when it’s been shut up too long.

With the house, it’s more like—I see one thing crooked, then I see the next thing, and then I remember my uncle had a porch that leaned like that, out near where we used to go when I was a kid… but no, I’m not sure I’m doing it to get rid of something. It just gets my attention, I guess. Once I start looking at it, the rest of the world kind of slips off to the side.
